We introduce the Quantitative Kinematics Benchmark (QK-Bench), comprising 2,341 multiple-choice QA pairs from 379 videos and 17 tasks spanning Object-Centric Kinematics, Camera Ego-Motion Kinematics, and Multi-Object Kinematic Relations. Hidden geometric and temporal signals support trajectory construction and filtering, while fixed kinematic rules map physical quantities to verifiable answers, including categorical labels. Trajectory and question validation ensure target reliability and sufficient visual support in the sampled frames. All models receive a unified monocular RGB input. Evaluations of proprietary, open-source, and spatial MLLMs reveal substantial limitations: the strongest model achieves only 41.2\% overall accuracy, and spatial MLLMs show no consistent advantage. These findings indicate that current advances in spatial intelligence do not reliably transfer to quantitative kinematic understanding.



### ScaleCurve: From Constant Scale to Consistent Motion for World-Space Human Mesh Recovery
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34005v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34005v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 08:51:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 08:51:38+00:00
- **Authors**: Sanghyeon Lee, Jong Taek Lee
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: World-space human mesh recovery can lose spatial consistency even when camera-space body estimates are accurate. A single sequence-wide scale resolves global scale ambiguity but cannot correct local scale distortion along a reconstructed camera path. We present ScaleCurve, a framework that estimates time-varying metric corrections from human-ground geometry. We construct scale observations by comparing predicted foot geometry with locally fitted ground planes. Joint temporal optimization downweights inconsistent observations and couples neighboring scale estimates, allowing calibration to adapt along the camera path without freely following measurement noise. We scale camera translation increments and integrate them into a calibrated trajectory. With this camera fixed, we refine the lifted body motion using 2D image evidence and pretrained 3D dynamics, while restricting global trajectory rescaling. ScaleCurve achieves state-of-the-art performance on most evaluated metrics on both EMDB-2 and SLOPER4D. Consistent gains across three HMR backbones demonstrate its compatibility with diverse body estimates without retraining. Code will be made publicly available.



### Manga Page Parser: Comprehensive Visual Parsing through Local Story Understanding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34006v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34006v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 08:53:56+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 08:53:56+00:00
- **Authors**: Yingxuan Li, Jiafeng Mao, Arata Furukawa, Mayu Otani
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Understanding manga requires connecting who appears and who speaks with the events, character states, and relationships that unfold across panels. We study manga visual parsing through local story understanding: analyzing each double-page spread independently to capture and organize its story information in a reusable structured representation. We propose MangaParser, an agentic reading framework that jointly performs character clustering, speaker diarization, and speaker-to-character binding within a shared multimodal conversation, without additional training or fine-tuning. The agent uses structured story memory to guide character and speaker identification, inspecting relevant image regions when uncertain and refining its predictions and story records together as evidence accumulates. Quantitative evaluations show that our parser substantially outperforms manga-specific models across multiple backbones. Parsing examples illustrate how a unified representation links the character and speaker predictions with narrative content and supporting evidence. We further test reusability through story question answering using outputs generated before the questions are provided. The results confirm output reusability, offering a foundation for manga translation, content retrieval, and question answering.



### Rethinking Unsupervised Cross-Modal Homography Estimation:Spatially Aware Representations and Geometric Consistency Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34021v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34021v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:07:55+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:07:55+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhuoran Yang, Junchen Yu, Runmin Zhang, Linshan Li, Chenghao Zhang, Hui-liang Shen, Si-Yuan Cao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Unsupervised cross-modal homography estimation is challenging because appearance differences obscure shared geometric structures. Separating modality transfer from homography estimation helps reduce these differences, but accurate alignment still requires effective geometric representations and consistency constraints on the two subtasks. We propose GeoCo (Geometry-Aware and Consistency-Constrained Homography Estimation), a framework that addresses these two requirements without cross-modal transformation labels. Its homography estimator, RVHomo, adapts hierarchical features from a frozen vision foundation model (VFM) and fuses them with local CNN features to capture structural context and spatial detail. Two geometric consistency constraints encourage modality transfer to preserve geometry and homography predictions to remain consistent under synthetic geometric transformations. Through two-stage progressive distillation, we further obtain GeoCo-D, which uses the lightweight RVHomo-D estimator. An intermediate model first learns to perform cross-modal matching without modality transfer, then transfers prediction and feature knowledge to the student, eliminating online VFM extraction. Experiments on GoogleMap, OPT-SAR, FSD, and VIS-IR show that GeoCo reduces mean average corner error by 17.4-46.7% relative to the best-performing baseline on each dataset. GeoCo-D also outperforms all compared baselines while using 98.5% fewer parameters and 70.4% fewer FLOPs than the complete GeoCo teacher.



### MoSk: A Unified Motion-Skeleton Foundation Model for Human Motion Understanding and Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34022v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34022v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:09:12+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:09:12+00:00
- **Authors**: Yun Chen, Munchurl Kim, Jeonghyeok Do
- **Comment**: Please visit our project page at https://kaist-viclab.github.io/MoSk_site
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Foundation models (FMs) have advanced visual understanding and generation by providing semantically rich representations. However, this role of FMs remains underexplored in human motion. Large motion datasets offer diverse movements at scale, but often lack action labels for systematically evaluating learned representations on understanding tasks. Skeleton datasets enable such evaluation through established recognition protocols, yet are typically smaller and focus on predefined actions captured in controlled environments. To combine these strengths, we present MoSk, to the best of our knowledge, the first Motion-Skeleton foundation model to integrate motion and skeleton datasets. MoSk learns shared representations through self-supervised masked motion modeling. We incorporate joint-name embeddings to provide a common anatomical reference across various skeleton formats. A cross-attention decoder reconstructs masked motion from the encoder's features, with joint-name embeddings specifying the target joints. Experiments demonstrate strong action-recognition performance across diverse settings, including linear evaluation and fine-tuning. MoSk also substantially improves motion generation across multiple backbones through representation alignment, reducing FID on MotionMillion by up to 9.75 for unconditional generation and 3.83 for text-to-motion generation.



### Incorporating the Knowledge Distillation to Improve the EfficientNet Transfer Learning Capability
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34025v1
- **DOI**: 10.1109/ICoDSA50139.2020.9212994
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34025v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:12:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:12:38+00:00
- **Authors**: Tjeng Wawan Cenggoro
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: Cenggoro, Tjeng Wawan. "Incorporating the knowledge distillation to improve the efficientnet transfer learning capability." 2020 International Conference on Data Science and its Applications (ICoDSA). IEEE, 2020
- **Summary**: Due to the Deep Learning requirement for a large training dataset, Transfer Learning has become a central method in the field of Computer Vision, which heavily used Deep Learning. Since the adoption of transfer learning in the field, the performance of the models in computer vision is significantly upgraded. The common transfer learning practice in computer vision research is to use state-of-the-art architectures in the ImageNet dataset as the backbone network. The best performing architecture in ImageNet is currently held by EfficientNet, which is a set of architectures that were progressively grown from a baseline architecture.   By observing the EfficientNet development approach, it is natural to hypothesize that the smaller EfficientNet architecture is capable to contain the knowledge of the larger architecture. This hypothesis comes from the fact that the larger EfficientNet architecture was grown from the smaller architecture. Therefore, in this study, we experimented if it is beneficial to transfer the knowledge from the larger architecture to the smaller architecture of the EfficientNet. To achieve this goal, we proposed a transfer learning method that uses Knowledge Distillation as the knowledge transfer mechanism from the larger to smaller architecture. We found that the proposed method is able to upgrade the performance of each of the EfficientNet architecture. Several smaller architectures are even able to outperform the larger architecture that was trained using the standard transfer learning method.



### ContextHands: Completing Hand Motion with Learned Context and Joint-Specific Body Kinematics
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34027v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34027v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:13:29+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:13:29+00:00
- **Authors**: Taewoo Kim, Seunghun Koh, Hyosup Shin, Sungho Jo
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 3 figures. Main paper only
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Detailed hand motion contributes to the realism and expressiveness of generated human motion. Completing these details from body motion and language requires connecting the context of an action to the articulation of the hands. We introduce ContextHands, which completes hand motion from an observed body sequence and text descriptions assigned to hands and time intervals. Our approach learns probabilistic hand features from the descriptions and body motion, using auxiliary hand prediction to connect these features to hand articulation. A body encoder preserves joint and body-part features across multiple temporal scales, allowing each hand joint to query these features for joint-specific body information. The diffusion generator combines this joint-specific body information with the learned hand context. Both encoders and the generator are trained jointly, so the final generation objective also shapes the context features. Experiments on the OakInk2 dataset show that ContextHands generates more accurate hand motion and achieves lower Fréchet Hand Distance than existing hand-motion and general human-motion generation methods adapted to the task. In qualitative examples of grasping and tool use, ContextHands more closely reproduces the detailed hand poses in the reference motion. We will release our code and pretrained models to support further research.



### Counter-DECEIVR: Counterfactual Explanations for Deepfake Detectors using Detector-Guided Rectified Flow Inversion
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34032v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34032v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:16:25+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:16:25+00:00
- **Authors**: Mariano V. Ntrougkas, Vasileios Mezaris, Ioannis Patras
- **Comment**: Under review
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Deep-learning-generated media are increasingly passed off as real, driving an arms race between image generators and deepfake detectors. Trust in the latter is limited by their often black-box nature; and, while region-based explanations can sometimes be extracted, these become less useful as generation quality improves. Counterfactual examples, which show the minimal change that flips a detector's decision, reveal what evidence drives a verdict, helping analysts decide whether to trust it. This work introduces Counter-DECEIVR (DEtector-guided reCtifiEd flow InVeRsion for counterfactuals), a novel training-free framework that generates counterfactual examples. Counter-DECEIVR steers latents in the velocity field of a rectified flow model using gradients from off-the-shelf deepfake detectors, without training a generator or adapting the detector; unlike prior methods, it requires no per-instance or per-detector fitting and no diffusion training on the target data. It also leverages the detector's explanation maps to concentrate editing to salient regions, turning low-level feature importance into localised counterfactual edits. A single parameter controls the strength of the edits. Across two deepfake detectors and two widely used forensics datasets, Counter-DECEIVR outperforms prior counterfactual generation methods, including training-based ones. Code will be made available upon acceptance.



### End-to-End Image-Goal Navigation for Agile Quadrotors via Recurrent State Distillation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34046v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34046v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:23:33+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:23:33+00:00
- **Authors**: Simone Nascivera, Ismail Geles, Davide Scaramuzza
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Image-goal navigation asks a robot to reach the viewpoint of a target photograph without a map or goal pose. In three dimensions, this requires not only recognizing the goal but also estimating 3D displacement and generating continuous control actions to approach and settle at the target view. This is difficult because the same visual difference between current and goal image can require very different actions depending on the robot's velocity, yet monocular images alone reveal neither metric scale nor body motion. As a result, existing image-goal policies are limited to planar action spaces and rely on a state estimator for flight control. Policies that map directly from images to low-level control commands have been demonstrated for agile flight, but not toward goal images in unseen environments. We present the first image-goal navigation policy that directly commands thrust and angular rates for agile quadrotor flight in unseen 3D environments, without relying on state estimation (e.g., SLAM, VO, or SfM) or a separate velocity controller. We train a recurrent vision-based policy through a four-stage pipeline that combines geometric and motion supervision with privileged-teacher distillation and end-to-end reinforcement learning fine-tuning. On 1000 held-out flights in unseen environments, the policy achieves an 82.3% success rate with a mean terminal position error of 0.10m across successful flights. Deployed zero-shot on a real quadrotor, the policy reaches an 84% success rate over 25 trials in an unseen indoor environment.



### Understanding Diffusion Acceleration: Methods, Trade-Offs, and Open Questions
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34056v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34056v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:32:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:32:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Xiaofeng Mao, Fu-yun Wang, Le Zhuo, Zhengkai Jiang, Shaohao Rui, Weijie Ma
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Diffusion and flow-based generative models support high-quality image, audio, and video synthesis. Sampling can require dozens to hundreds of denoiser evaluations, with each pass becoming more expensive as resolution, video length, or model size increases. This cost affects latency and energy use in interactive and edge applications. We examine four approaches to accelerating inference: distillation, consistency training, sampling methods, and denoiser design. We express training objectives, transition maps, and numerical updates in a common notation to compare changes to the learning target, the sampling trajectory, and the work performed within each denoiser pass. Published image and video benchmarks illustrate the quality--cost trade-offs of one- and few-step generation, the interaction between solvers and timestep schedules, the effect of feature caching, and the difference between first-frame latency and sustained streaming throughput. We close with open problems in visual-native architectures and post-compression distillation, solver-aware few-step training, and semantic trajectory alignment with factorized distribution matching.



### Surf-Style: Surface-Based Scene Stylization
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34062v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34062v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:36:20+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:36:20+00:00
- **Authors**: Wanrong Hong, Ancheng Lin, Mukesh Prasad, Jun Li
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 6 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Consistent and controllable 3D stylization is critical for visual computing applications such as immersive visualization, augmented and virtual reality content creation, and artistic scene rendering. While 2D image stylization has advanced significantly, extending these methods to 3D remains challenging due to the need for cross-view consistency, where stylized renderings must align across all viewpoints. Applying 2D stylization independently to each rendered view often results in inconsistencies across perspectives. Existing methods attempt to improve consistency by leveraging implicit radiance field representations, which model scene appearance as a function of spatial locations. However, these methods struggle with ambiguity in updating colors, as the indirect dependence between pixel appearance and learnable scene parameters makes it difficult to enforce consistent stylization during optimization. This work presents Surface-based Scene Stylization (Surf-Style), which reconstructs object surfaces in a 3D scene and uses these surfaces to achieve geometric consistency and localized stylization. Surf-Style defines a learnable appearance model over 3D space, which is modified to replicate the visual style of a reference image. Compared to state-of-the-art methods, our method achieves superior consistency and offers greater flexibility in controllable stylization, including the ability to target specific surface regions and combine different styles.



### SPARC: Geometry-Guided Attention Pruning for Long-Horizon Camera-Controlled Video Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34064v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34064v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:38:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:38:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Zesong Yang, Weikai Chen, Liyuan Cui, Yuan Li, Jiaxin Huang, Lutao Jiang, Runze Zhang, Yingda Yin, Xiaoyang Huang, Kai Yan, Keyang Luo, Xin Wang, Hujun Bao, Zhaopeng Cui
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We present SPARC, a geometry-guided attention pruning framework that turns the native self-attention of pretrained video diffusion transformers into a unified mechanism for camera control and persistent scene memory. Our key insight is that attention connectivity can simultaneously serve as geometric conditioning for camera control and spatial addressing for historical memory retrieval. Motivated by the asymmetric contextual requirements of reconstructing observed content and synthesizing novel regions, SPARC restricts observed tokens to geometrically corresponding historical tokens and local spatiotemporal context, while preserving dense attention for unobserved regions. Correspondence-aware attention biasing and order-invariant historical positioning further enable stable retrieval across long temporal gaps. Without introducing additional learnable control or memory modules, SPARC achieves precise camera adherence and faithful scene revisitation while substantially reducing attention computation. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of aggressive attention pruning in improving camera adherence, revisit consistency, and visual fidelity, with an inference speedup of over 50x when combined with four-step distillation. Project page is available at https://zju3dv.github.io/SPARC.



### When to Respond, Where to Ground: Streaming 3D Affordance Grounding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34067v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34067v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:39:19+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:39:19+00:00
- **Authors**: Wenting Luan, Weilong Yan, Siqi Lu, Ming Li, Beibei Lin, Yongbin Zheng, Robby T. Tan
- **Comment**: 19 pages, 6 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Given a continuous RGB-D stream and a language request, streaming 3D affordance grounding requires an agent to decide when to respond and where to locate the requested functional part using only observations available so far. Existing 3D affordance grounding methods typically assume complete scene observations, while streaming vision-language models lack fine-grained 3D functional grounding. Our goal is to jointly determine response timing and functional-part localization during continuous scene observation. We propose StreamAff, an evidence-guided response strategy that makes both decisions from the grounding model's own visual evidence. The model scores the visual patches of the frames it reads: their combined support, weighed against a wait option, decides when to respond, and the highest-scoring patch selects the frame to ground in. A conditional look-back policy lets the model answer from an earlier observation at the moment of the request or wait for new evidence, and zoom-and-refine grounding segments the part named in the answer on the 3D points of the selected frame. We further introduce StreamFun3D, a benchmark derived from real-world 3D scenes that evaluates response timing and grounding across four evidence settings: Current, History, Future, and Unobserved, together with StreamIoU, a metric that credits a mask only when the response is timely and grounded in a valid source frame. We adapt offline 3D affordance grounding methods and build external-decider baselines that pair vision-language models with our mask decoder. StreamAff achieves 30.14% StreamIoU on 15-second streams, a 40.6% relative improvement over the strongest of these baselines.



### Hijacking 3D Generation: Targeted Adversarial Attacks against Image-to-3D Models via Prefix Trajectory Alignment
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34075v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34075v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:45:09+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:45:09+00:00
- **Authors**: Yilong Yang, Jiale Teng, Yule Liu, Yang Liu, Xinlei He, Zhuo Ma
- **Comment**: 18 pages, 10 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Online services that deploy Image-to-3D models enable users to generate high-quality 3D assets from a single image. However, the generated 3D geometry can violate content policies and cause broader security risks. To prevent such content, these services typically use policy-restricted inspectors to examine uploaded images before generation. Since the inspector examines only the visible content of the input, a visually benign image can still induce the generation of policy-restricted 3D geometry. However, turning this vulnerability into an effective attack is non-trivial in the black-box setting: global backpropagation through the long DiT generation trajectory is difficult to fit, while different image preprocessing pipelines across services can substantially reduce transferability. In this paper, we present PTA3D, a targeted adversarial attack against Image-to-3D models that addresses these challenges through Prefix Trajectory Alignment (PTA) and Preprocessing Aware Optimization (PAO). PTA progressively aligns the prefix DiT outputs of the adversarial trajectory with those of the target trajectory, where the core geometric structure is formed, allowing the final geometry to be controlled without optimizing through the complete generation trajectory. PAO exposes adversarial optimization to diverse image transformations rather than a single preprocessing pipeline, reducing sensitivity to the mismatch between surrogate and target services. We establish a prefix-to-output bound and a preprocessing-aware transfer bound to theoretically characterize the effectiveness of the two designs. Experiments on three open-source Image-to-3D models and three online services show that PTA3D achieves 41.5%-88.5% ASR on online services and 90.8%-94.6% ASR in white box settings, substantially outperforming existing attacks.



### Projector Subspace Optimization for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34078v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34078v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:48:43+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:48:43+00:00
- **Authors**: Hao Yu, Quan Cheng, Lijun Zhang
- **Comment**: 29 pages, 12 figures, including supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance through instruction tuning, yet adapting them to sequential tasks requires Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT). Existing replay-free methods expand prompts or routed LoRA experts across multi-layer decoders, incurring heavy parameter and routing overhead while shifting internal feature distributions. Instead, we identify the final linear layer of the vision-language projector as a high-capacity, coordinate-stationary bridge, where sequential updates easily induce interface drift by displacing historical visual tokens. While low-rank subspace adaptation offers a compact remedy, selecting directions solely from historical statistics or task gradients sacrifices plasticity or stability, and unconstrained multi-step updates still accumulate large drift. To resolve both issues, we propose Projector Refinement through Optimized Update Directions (PROUD), a continual subspace optimization framework for the terminal projector layer. At task onset, PROUD combines retained input statistics with initial gradients to analytically extract a metric-orthonormal subspace maximizing first-order task gain under a drift budget. During training, it regularizes updates within this fixed subspace via an exact Frobenius penalty before losslessly merging them with zero inference or routing overhead. Experiments on UCIT and CoIN across MLLM backbones show that PROUD outperforms recent replay-free baselines in continual accuracy while curbing forgetting with a fraction of trainable parameters.



### WorldBridge: Bridging Scattered Views for Coherent 3D World Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34079v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34079v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:48:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:48:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Weilong Yan, Jingyu Hu, Hongli Xu, Yifan Zhan, Beibei Lin, Anbang Wang, Qinqian Lei, Jingwei Huang, Robby T. Tan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Generating coherent and explorable 3D worlds from scattered images with little or no visual overlap is a fundamental and long-standing challenge in computer vision, which existing geometry-aware methods fail to address. Meanwhile, existing image- and video-based methods may generate unseen regions but produce inconsistent geometry across views. To bridge this gap, we present WorldBridge, a framework for coherent and controllable 3D world generation under scattered images with little or no visual overlap. WorldBridge encodes scattered references into a global scene context to guide geometry and appearance generation across views. Our temporal and spatial reference attention mechanism further enhances the fidelity of temporal references along the target trajectory and supports flexible spatial references outside it. A plug-and-play latent-reference consistency strategy improves reference fidelity through reprojection-guided test-time optimization. We also introduce the WorldBridge benchmark suite to evaluate 3D world generation from scattered, low-overlap references, which is rarely explored by current benchmarks. Both qualitative and quantitative results demonstrate that our method enables controllable and coherent 3D world generation, surpassing the existing state-of-the-art solutions. Our project page is available at https://davidyan2001.github.io/WorldBridge/.



### MulCLIP: A Multi-level Alignment Framework for Enhancing Fine-grained Long-context CLIP
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34083v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.IR**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34083v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:53:13+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:53:13+00:00
- **Authors**: Chau Truong, Hieu Ta Quang, Zhenzhen Liu, Dung D. Le
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-language models such as CLIP excel at image-text alignment but struggle with long, detailed descriptions due to training on short captions. Recent methods address this limitation using region proposals to align visual regions with sentences, but at substantial deployment cost. We present MulCLIP, an end-to-end multi-level alignment framework that directly exploits natural long-text structure without region proposals. Instead of simply stacking objectives, MulCLIP aligns different textual granularities through compatible training signals: global contrastive alignment for long captions and short summaries, Word-Patch Reconstruction over locally calibrated features for within-sample word-patch semantics, and Subcaption-Aggregated Patch alignment for context-rich subcaption grounding. Experiments on benchmarks spanning varying caption lengths show consistent gains, and ablations confirm that the proposed objectives are complementary and jointly improve the overall framework. Code will be released at https://github.com/truongchau2602/MulCLIP.



### TaCo: Rethinking Correlation for Visual Tracking on Edge Devices
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34084v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34084v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 09:57:40+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 09:57:40+00:00
- **Authors**: Qingmao Wei, Fagui Liu, Quan Tang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Lightweight trackers have replaced cross-correlation with attention, and on low-power edge hardware they pay for it, since memory-bound softmax, normalization and reshaping operators of attention cost far more time than their arithmetic suggests. Benchmarking efficient trackers on an edge Neural Processing Unit (NPU) and an Arm CPU under one numerically validated protocol, we find that correlation trackers are the fast ones, but they lag in accuracy unless they adopt a ViT backbone, which brings attention back. We revisit what correlation lacks. Template--search interaction serves two roles: conditioning the search features on the target and matching them against it spatially. Attention does both, whereas correlation only matches, and does so on features extracted without knowledge of the target. We present TaCo (Target-aligned Correlation), which supplies the missing conditioning without attention by letting the template enter the per-frame network only as operands computed once per video. A descriptor of the target scales and shifts the search features channel by channel inside the backbone, and kernels aligned with the target box, each with a weight predicted from the template, correlate with them in one matrix product. With the backbone fixed, our correlation remains essential while incurring negligible NPU overhead, offering a better speed-accuracy trade-off than the attention-based interactions we tested. TaCo-T achieves 62.3% LaSOT AUC at 184 FPS on the Rockchip RV1126B NPU and 96 FPS on a Raspberry Pi 5 CPU, 3.0x and 3.5x the NPU speed of HiT-Tiny and AsymTrack-T at higher accuracy. TaCo-B reaches 65.6% at 50 FPS on the NPU, more than twice the speed of HiT-Base, AsymTrack-B and LightFC-vit, again at higher accuracy. Code and models are available at https://github.com/TsingWei/TaCo-Tracker.



### From Diagnosing Alignment to Joint Supervision in Multimodal Draft Training
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34091v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34091v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:00:08+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:00:08+00:00
- **Authors**: Haozhe Du, Runze Liu, Jiaming Fan, Yitong Tao, Shuxia Lin, Chonghan Liu, Daming Cao, Xu Yang
- **Comment**: 18 pages, 6 figures, 10 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal drafts learn from multimodal (MM) and text-only (TXT) supervision, yet their effects on alignment and how to combine them remain unclear. We diagnose draft-target alignment through paired predictions with and without visual input, decomposing distributional error into text-conditioned and visual-response components. Drafts with similar text-conditioned alignment can still differ markedly in visual-response risk and acceptance. Controlled continuation training shows that high-risk groups outperform random and low-risk groups, and visual-response risk guides MM training more effectively than aggregate risk. Building on these diagnostics, we propose MNRS-Joint, which configures joint supervision from a given draft's component-risk distributions, using modality-specific mean risks to determine the MM:TXT mixture. Across three warm-ups, its distinct mixtures fall in high-performing regions of the ratio sweeps. On LLaVA-1.5-7B, MNRS-Joint improves mean accepted length from 3.35 to 3.53 and mean speedup from $2.04\times$ to $2.16\times$ over composition-matched random training under greedy tree decoding. The gains also hold under stochastic chain decoding.



### Exploring the Design Factors of Pixel-space Diffusion
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34093v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34093v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:02:35+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:02:35+00:00
- **Authors**: Mang Ning, Le Zhang, Albert Ali Salah, Itir Onal Ertugrul, Jintao Zhang
- **Comment**: 11 pages
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In this paper, we introduce EiT by exploring the key design factors of pixel-space diffusion from three perspectives: model architecture, representation, and frequency domain. Inspired by Denoising Autoencoders (DAE) which perform denoising through a bottleneck, we construct a U-shaped pixel diffusion transformer that performs explicit compression along the channel dimension. In terms of representation, we find that both pixel and latent diffusion transformers exhibit a three-stage representation dynamics across layers and timesteps: denoising/local feature expansion, basis lift-up, and posterior projection. Moreover, earlier denoising and a longer basis lift-up stage are associated with better generation, which EiT naturally achieves through its channel bottleneck and increased network depth. From the frequency perspective, we establish the equivalence between pixel-space and frequency-space diffusion when keeping the network operating in pixel space. On ImageNet $256\times256$ and $512\times512$, EiT consistently outperforms JiT (Just image Transformers) in generation quality. Specifically, our EiT achieves better FID than JiT: 1.61 vs. 1.85 at $256\times256$, and 1.73 vs. 1.99 at $512\times512$. Remarkably, EiT can be seamlessly applied to video pixel diffusion by simply extending the patch size to temporal dimension and outperforms video latent diffusion in FVD evaluated on Kinetics-600 (resolution 256), under comparable total network parameters. Code is available at https://github.com/forever208/EiT



### FoMNet: Adaptive Kernels Meet Contextual Matching for Cross-Modal Homography Estimation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34096v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34096v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:03:32+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:03:32+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhuoran Yang, Zhenyu Yi, Runmin Zhang, Linshan Li, Yiming Li, Xiaokai Bai, Xiaohan Zhang, Hui-liang Shen, Si-Yuan Cao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Cross-modal homography estimation requires reliable correspondences despite appearance differences between sensing modalities. Multi-scale refinement progressively reduces the search range, but its accuracy remains limited by the ability of matching features to distinguish nearby candidates. The vision foundation model (VFM) provides complementary representations, yet integrating these features through fixed interpolation leaves their spatial aggregation independent of image content and the correspondence objective. We propose the Foundation Model-Enhanced Matching Network (FoMNet), which combines learned spatial adaptation of VFM features with contextual correspondence estimation. Patch Feature Upsampling (PFU) progressively predicts location-specific kernels to aggregate neighboring features on upsampled grids, preparing VFM representations for fusion with CNN features at different matching scales. Contextual Correlation Refinement (CCR) then jointly processes source descriptors and local correlations with spatial context to refine candidate scores before each homography update. To reduce upsampling overhead, we develop FoMNet-D, which replaces PFU with bicubic interpolation and uses multi-scale feature-relation distillation to recover accuracy. Experiments on five cross-modal datasets show that FoMNet achieves the lowest mean average corner error (MACE) among the evaluated methods, reducing MACE by 10.6%-72.7% relative to MCNet. FoMNet-D retains competitive accuracy while reducing inference latency by 34.0% relative to FoMNet.



### When Position Favors the Wrong Part: Evaluating Left-Right Discrimination in Semantic Correspondence
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34100v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34100v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:07:58+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:07:58+00:00
- **Authors**: Pingan Wang
- **Comment**: 9 figures & 11 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Overall part-discrimination accuracy can conceal failures when image position favors the wrong bilateral part. We examine whether dense descriptors distinguish the correct part under this conflict and whether their discrimination scores help select matching coordinates. We evaluate position agreement and conflict within the same target images and annotated part pairs, using a bounding-box-side score to define the conditions. We compare the original descriptors with their even and odd components under horizontal reflection, examine feature upsampling with the backbone and adapters fixed, and test candidate-selection rules based on odd scores. On SPair-71k, odd discrimination accuracy falls from 86.66% under agreement to 19.63% under conflict for frozen DINOv2, compared with 95.27% to 86.16% for MARCO. With MARCO's input geometry fixed, odd accuracy in box-side conflict was 34.12% on the native grid and 88.84% after learned upsampling and pooling back to that grid. Odd-score rules improve on the original hard-peak matchers overall for DINOv2 and DINOv3 and on conflict queries for MARCO. With MARCO's soft decoder fixed, reranking the cosine peaks changes mean per-pair PCK by +0.47 pp. For supervised GeoAware-SC, using the odd score to select the localization window center yields only 15 net additional correct matches, with 330 corrections and 315 introduced errors. Evaluating discrimination under position conflict reveals failures hidden by aggregate accuracy. Reporting corrections and introduced errors separately also shows whether a discriminative score produces useful matching decisions.



### What Makes a Gavagai? Learning Visual Concepts from One Positive and Few Negatives
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34105v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34105v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:12:13+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:12:13+00:00
- **Authors**: Katsuya Ogata, Kazuya Nishimura, Yuta Nakashima
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Humans can recognize novel concepts from only a few observations by identifying defining visual attributes and eliminating alternative interpretations. However, a single observation is consistent with multiple possible concepts, and accurate visual perception alone is insufficient to determine the intended interpretation. Negative evidence can reduce this ambiguity by eliminating competing hypotheses. Inspired by Quine's gavagai problem, we introduce the Gavagai Benchmark to evaluate whether vision-language models can identify the attributes defining a novel concept from positive and negative examples. The benchmark consists of synthetic images depicting novel combinations of visual attributes and real images of newly described species. Using these datasets, we examine how models use negative examples to identify novel concepts and analyze the respective roles of visual perception and reasoning. Several models struggle even when correct attributes are provided, while others show large gaps when ground-truth attributes are replaced with model predictions. These diagnostic comparisons highlight challenges in both attribute perception and inference from limited evidence.



### Detectable by Design: Learning a Continuous 3D Saliency Field from 2D Detector Responses
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34108v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34108v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:12:47+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:12:47+00:00
- **Authors**: Han Sun, Jun Li, Christy Jie Liang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: The 3D points kept in a map decide whether a camera can be localized against it, and this choice matters more as the map becomes sparser. Existing 3D keypoint detectors usually define saliency by semantics, repeatability, reconstruction or descriptor matchability. None of these definitions asks whether a point will actually be detected in an image, and as a result most of the points they select are not detected again in images.   In this paper, we define 3D saliency as image detectability: the response of a 2D interest-point detector at a surface point, aggregated over all views in which the point is visible. We propose DetField, a continuous field over 3D space that takes a point cloud as input and predicts detectability at any location around it. The supervision is the response of a 2D detector, back-projected from images onto the point cloud. Multi-scale curvature invariants are passed to the decoder at every query point, bypassing the voxel pooling of the encoder.   In a localization benchmark that isolates the choice of 3D points, DetField outperforms Harris-3D and five learned 3D keypoint methods on objects and on real indoor scenes. On unseen rooms, it improves localization success by 7.4 points over Harris-3D, the strongest baseline. On objects, it also leads under a 2D detector not used in training. On sparse maps, it stays ahead of Harris-3D on both objects and real scenes.



### A Simple Yet Effective Agent for Open-World Aerial Object Search
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34115v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34115v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:17:35+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:17:35+00:00
- **Authors**: Jitai Hao, Liangxing Yang, Zhenyang Li, Fan Liu, Qiang Huang, Jun Yu
- **Comment**: 20 pages, 10 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Open-world aerial object search methods often rely on persistent spatial maps, learned world models, or task-specific policy training. To examine how far a minimal, training-free design can go, we introduce SimpleUAV, an agent built around a frozen, off-the-shelf vision-language model (VLM). SimpleUAV separates current-view target assessment from history-conditioned action selection, combining fresh candidate evidence with lightweight search context. Local geometry converts visual selections into bounded straight-line movements, depth-based checks halt blocked segments, and measured execution feedback guides subsequent decisions. This closed loop sustains search without persistent spatial maps or task-specific training. SimpleUAV achieves 41.61% Success Rate (SR) on UAV-ON and 11.98% on AerialDojo-200K, compared with 23.80% and 3.20% for the strongest prior baselines, respectively. With limited benchmark-specific adaptation, the same core agent transfers to aerial Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) on TravelUAV, improving Full SR by at least 19.66% across all three splits under L1 assistance. These results establish a strong baseline for evaluating when additional architectural complexity benefits aerial search.



### Out of Sight, Not Out of Clues: Can Video LLMs Guess from Partial Evidence?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34126v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.CL
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34126v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:30:08+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:30:08+00:00
- **Authors**: Luoxi Zhang, Jiawei Liu, Yanlin Luo, Peiyang He, Junlin Zhu, Chun To Choi, Yang Xiong, Jiang Wu, Xingyu Zeng, Min Yang, Shiwen Ni
- **Comment**: 27 pages, 21 figures, 23 tables. Code: https://github.com/suat-llm/ClueProbe
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In real-world applications like egocentric recording, visual accessibility, and surveillance review, visual evidence for Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs) is often only partially captured. A reliable model should match its response to the evidence: answering when sufficient, guessing from visible clues when partial, and abstaining when absent. Yet existing benchmarks enforce a binary answerable-or-unanswerable split, leaving partial evidence unmeasured and obscuring whether failures arise from missing evidence or misjudging sufficiency. We introduce ClueProbe, a diagnostic benchmark to (1) separate perceiving evidence from judging sufficiency, and (2) validate our diagnosis via targeted fine-tuning. ClueProbe comprises 1,721 human-annotated questions across 598 natural videos, labeled Answerable, Guessable, or Unanswerable, alongside controlled perturbations altering wording or visual input. Evaluating 10 open-source and proprietary Video LLMs reveals that failures stem from judgment rather than perception: models perceive the evidence yet lack graded sufficiency awareness, answering or refusing outright while rarely guessing from partial clues. Targeted fine-tuning shows that this reflects missing supervision rather than insufficient capacity: supervising the guess level lifts appropriate guessing from 1.6% to 40.4% without extra false refusals, whereas conventional refusal tuning rejects 92% of guessable questions. Data and code will be released at https://github.com/suat-llm/ClueProbe.



### FoundSurface: Feedforward Ray-Based 3D Scene Surface Reconstruction from Unposed Images
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34128v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.CG, cs.GR, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34128v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:30:24+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:30:24+00:00
- **Authors**: Shenxing Wei, Jinxi Li, Zihui Zhang, Ajay Kumar, Wei Wang, Bo Yang
- **Comment**: NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://vlar-group.github.io/FoundSurface.html
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Accurate 3D surface reconstruction from unposed images remains a fundamental challenge in computer vision. Recent feed-forward pointmap methods eliminate the need for camera poses but inherently produce discrete, sparse representations. In this paper, we present a generalizable pipeline that directly recovers continuous, high-fidelity 3D surfaces from unposed images by seamlessly integrating pointmap- and ray-based representations. Our approach first extracts explicit geometric priors using a pre-trained pointmap model. A query-view-centric selection module then identifies geometrically relevant reference views via a robust surfel voting mechanism. Next, a cross-attention regressor bridges target query rays with reference features to estimate a continuous coarse surface. Lastly, a raylet-based module aggregates local 3D features to carve out high-frequency details and analytical normals. Trained as a single foundation model on 9 diverse datasets, our method demonstrates exceptional zero-shot generalization on 6 unseen datasets, clearly surpassing state-of-the-art baselines in geometric accuracy and detail preservation.



### A Calibration-Referenced Protocol for Measuring Decision Stability under Reference Bank Compression
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34134v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34134v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:33:48+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:33:48+00:00
- **Authors**: Yiwen Chen, Shengming Yu
- **Comment**: 54 pages, including supplementary material (Tables S1-S11 and Figures S1-S4). Interval summaries and probe-sensitivity CSV files are included as ancillary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reference banks used for industrial image inspection can be compressed to fit a deployment budget, but the resulting anomaly decisions depend on both the compressed scores and the operating threshold. We present a four-step protocol that compares compression methods at matched deployment sizes, establishes a reference distribution from 200 recalibrations, evaluates disagreement against two calibration benchmarks, and measures changes by distance from the full-bank threshold. With WideResNet-50, budget-matched product quantization (PQ) at the 10% budget has the lowest decision disagreement among five matched methods, including int8 scalar quantization, on VisA and MVTec AD at both operating levels. Int8 scalar quantization gives lower disagreement than PQ at the other four budgets. On MVTec AD at the lower operating level, PQ meets the calibration median in 34/39 cells and the upper quantile in 39/39 cells. Category-equally weighted image-level AUROC loss at this budget ranges from 0.0003 to 0.0037 across the evaluated configurations. On VisA at the 1% budget and a 1% operating level, disagreement reaches 28.12% (95% CI 23.25--33.58%) among near-threshold anomalies and 1.30% (0.79--2.14%) across the normal holdout. The 10% budget-matched PQ setting retains the lowest disagreement among four compared methods with ResNet-18, with clean reference banks, and when thresholds are estimated from normal-directory probes. The protocol connects deployment size to a calibration-referenced decision measure and identifies the evaluation populations most informative for inspection.



### An Image Detectability Field as a Viewpoint Utility for 2D-3D Localization in Sparse Maps
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34139v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34139v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:39:12+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:39:12+00:00
- **Authors**: Han Sun, Jun Li, Christy Jie Liang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In 2D-3D localization, a camera estimates its pose by matching an image to a 3D map. A camera held in a hand or mounted on a robot can often choose its viewpoint before it takes the image. In this work, we ask whether an image detectability field is a useful viewpoint utility when the planner reads only the geometry of the map. The field can be queried at any 3D point near a surface. From the local geometry around the point, it predicts how likely a 2D image detector is to fire at the projection of the point. We use the trained field as it is, without fine-tuning it for view selection. A fixed SuperPoint and EPnP-RANSAC localizer then estimates the pose from the chosen view. We answer in two settings, each with a test registered before the runs. On synthetic ShapeNet objects with sparse point maps, summing the field over the visible landmarks beats counting them by +2.0 points of localization success rate (object-clustered 95% interval [+0.7, +3.2]). It also beats summing Harris-3D ranks by +2.2 [+0.8, +3.5]. On real indoor scenes from SceneFun3D, our adaptation of the learned view scorer LWL falls below counting on the test rooms. We therefore add the field to a learned per-landmark planner. On the test rooms with sparse maps whose landmarks the field also selects, the planner with the field beats the same planner without it by +1.8 points. It beats the planner with Harris-3D or curvature in its place by +1.0 and +0.8 points. All three room-clustered intervals lie above zero. In both settings, the field helps in sparse maps and is on par with Harris-3D in dense maps.



### Benefit of Structured Representations: When Equivariance Helps in Image Classification
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34140v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34140v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:39:40+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:39:40+00:00
- **Authors**: Florian A. Hölzl, Daniel Rueckert, Georgios Kaissis
- **Comment**: NeurIPS 2026 Workshop NeurReps Proceedings
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Equivariant neural networks promise improved representation learning through structural guarantees. However, mainstream vision encoders rarely incorporate equivariance for most geometric symmetries. Images are a particularly interesting modality with many such symmetries and extensive datasets from which to learn this structure. In this work, we investigate when vision encoders benefit from geometric inductive biases. We study the discrete rotation group of quarter-turns (C4) which acts losslessly without interpolation or boundary artifacts. Combined with image classification as a controlled invariant objective, this allows for a principled empirical investigation into the role of structural guarantees. We introduce ConvNeXt-C4 as a rotation equivariant ConvNeXt with dimension-equivalent yet orientation-structured representations. Training from scratch, our experiments show, that the C4 variant outperforms its base model across all tasks while operating with 6x fewer parameters and 1.6x fewer FLOPs. Its performance advantage increases log-linearly with decreasing training set size, with pre-training providing a similar improvement across both model variants. Analyzing the classifier head reveals that orientation information from the representations' structure is actively used, improving performance by 4.90% across all datasets. Our results highlight the sample-efficiency benefits of equivariance and the value of retained orientation information, motivating further investigation beyond image classification. Code is available at https://github.com/hlzl/equivariant-convnext.



### Spe3DUp: Faithful Trajectory Distillation for Fast Image-to-3D Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34141v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34141v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:40:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:40:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Liangchao Xu, Jiaye Fu, Youtian Lin, Feihu Zhang, Yao Yao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent 3D generative models can produce high-quality meshes with complex structures and fine geometric details, with broad applications in games, film, and digital design. However, high-quality generation typically relies on long multi-step sampling processes that repeatedly evaluate large networks, leading to long inference times. At the same time, reference-driven tasks such as game-asset reconstruction and product digitization demand close correspondence to the input image: generated geometry must faithfully reconstruct the specific reference object. Acceleration must therefore reduce sampling computation while preserving the model's ability to reconstruct that object. To enable fast and faithful 3D generation, we introduce Spe3DUp, a coarse-to-fine framework based on Faithful Trajectory Distillation (FTD). FTD directly supervises few-step students with the teacher's input-conditioned denoising trajectories to preserve its generation behavior. We apply FTD separately to coarse geometry generation and geometric refinement, compressing both sampling processes while maintaining correspondence to the reference input. We further accelerate the complete mesh-generation pipeline with efficient geometry decoding and parallel remeshing. Experiments show that Spe3DUp maintains geometric quality and input correspondence close to those of the teacher, accelerates the coarse and refinement DiTs by 10.0x and 19.9x, respectively, and achieves a 7.7x end-to-end speedup over the original teacher pipeline.



### Non-Linear Motion from Two Frames: Cubic Hermite Trajectories for Video Frame Interpolation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34143v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34143v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:40:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:40:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Md Rifat Ur Rahman, Md Abtahi Majeed Chowdhury, Md Sakib Hossain Shovon, Jaesik Choi, Mohammad Ali Moni
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Two-frame video frame interpolation commonly resamples endpoint optical flow by the query time, which selects a constant-velocity straight-line path even though the same endpoint displacement is compatible with many intermediate trajectories. We present HermiteVFI, which replaces independent query-time motion prediction with one explicit continuous trajectory per pixel. The measured endpoint displacement fixes the two trajectory positions, while two latent endpoint derivatives learned from the frame pair control the interior path. A cubic Hermite curve is the minimum-degree polynomial that can independently satisfy these two position and two derivative boundary quantities. Its residual form contains linear motion exactly, structurally preserves the measured displacement endpoints for any network output, and has coefficients that are predicted once per frame pair and evaluated in closed form at arbitrary t. We further show that one interior query time gives a rank-deficient constraint on the two free trajectory coefficients, whereas any two distinct interior times are full rank at the representation level, motivating multi-time supervision. Appearance-derived directional cues help infer the latent derivatives that endpoint displacement alone cannot identify. On X4K1000FPS, SNU-FILM, and Vimeo90K-Septuplet, the largest gains occur for difficult non-uniform motion and high interpolation ratios. The amortization applies to trajectory-coefficient prediction; downstream reversal, refinement, and synthesis remain query dependent.



### Lost in the Grid: Where Multimodal LLMs Fail Between Seeing and Planning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34152v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34152v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:46:43+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:46:43+00:00
- **Authors**: Luoxi Zhang, Tianyi Tan, Jiawei Liu, Xingyu Zeng, Min Yang, Shiwen Ni
- **Comment**: 31 pages, 32 figures, 29 tables. Code: https://github.com/suat-llm/GridProbe
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) excel at static visual understanding and are increasingly applied to spatial planning, where a model interprets a visual layout and outputs an action sequence to reach a goal. Grid games are a common testbed for this ability because their rules are explicit and a solver can verify every move. Yet existing benchmarks mostly report end-to-end success rates, which hide whether a failure stems from visual perception, rule application, or multi-step planning. We introduce GridProbe, a diagnostic benchmark that 1) locates the failing stage between visual perception and planning and 2) verifies the diagnosis by targeted fine-tuning. On Sokoban, GridProbe pairs end-to-end solving with four probes (recognition, localization, single-step prediction, and state tracking) and gives every board both as an image and as an equivalent text grid. Evaluating seven MLLMs on Sokoban and a maze game reveals distinct bottlenecks behind similar success rates: proprietary models are limited by multi-step planning, whereas open-source models are limited by rule application or by visual grid indexing, i.e., counting rows and columns. Planning failures also hide perception failures: when planning is easier, the visual cost of the same model grows from 7.6 to 37.7 points. The diagnosis identifies what to train: fine-tuning only the vision module of the perception-limited model on board reading raises its interactive Sokoban success 3.5-fold (5.8% to 20.4%) and transfers zero-shot to the unseen maze game (single-turn success 13.8% to 67.1%), whereas the same data does not help a rule-limited model and training on solver solutions brings no gain. We will release the benchmark and code at https://github.com/suat-llm/GridProbe.



### HSTTA: Hierarchical Selective Test-Time Adaptation for Label-Free Cross-Subject EEG-to-Image Retrieval
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34153v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34153v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:47:33+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:47:33+00:00
- **Authors**: Hongzhou Chen, Yuhao Wei, Lianghua He, Yihang Liu, Zengqiang Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Cross-subject EEG-to-image retrieval suffers from substantial performance degradation on new subjects due to subject shift. Test-time adaptation (TTA) can mitigate this shift by adapting pretrained models with unlabeled target-subject EEG. However, most existing TTA methods struggle to match supervision granularity to each query's prediction confidence, leaving many queries without reliable pseudo-labels. To address this, we propose a novel framework named Hierarchical Selective Test-Time Adaptation (HSTTA). HSTTA builds a hierarchy over gallery image embeddings, uses hierarchical selective prediction to assign each EEG query a supervision granularity matching its current prediction confidence, and constructs adaptation objectives accordingly. We further propose Cross-Session Consistency Calibration (CSCC), an unsupervised method that calibrates the global confidence threshold to target a predefined accuracy level for hierarchical predictions. Moreover, we introduce Reciprocal Hierarchical Backoff (RHB), which moves queries lacking reverse-matching support to coarser nodes to mitigate error reinforcement. Experiments on two public benchmarks and ablation studies validate the effectiveness of HSTTA and its key components. On THINGS-EEG2, HSTTA outperforms the strongest EEG-specific TTA baseline by 17.9 (13.4) and 8.9 (3.7) percentage points in Top-1/Top-5 accuracy, respectively, under cosine (CSLS) ranking. Further analyses show that HSTTA yields more accurate pseudo-labels during adaptation, supporting more reliable model updates.



### OceanFix: Reference-Guided Diffusion for Sparse-View Underwater 3D Reconstruction
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34155v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34155v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:49:40+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:49:40+00:00
- **Authors**: Gen Li, Maoxin Chen, Yao Xiao, Xinyang Li, Yu Pan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent advances in Gaussian splatting have enabled efficient underwater reconstruction, but sparse observations, light attenuation, and backscatter still leave scene regions underconstrained and cause novel-view artifacts. We present OceanFix, a reference-guided framework for sparse-view underwater 3D reconstruction that combines diffusion-based view repair with Gaussian scene refinement. Our repair model follows a two-stage training strategy that separates underwater appearance adaptation from geometric refinement. The first stage learns underwater appearance from reference observations and estimated medium features. The second uses camera poses and reference depth to align reference features, with relative-depth supervision guiding structural repair without requiring target-view depth at inference. We further propose medium-aware distillation that transfers repaired views into the 3D scene according to geometric repair demand and channel-specific optical observability. Through iterative rendering, repair, and distillation, OceanFix refines underconstrained regions while retaining supervision from the observed views. Experiments on underwater datasets demonstrate that OceanFix improves rendering quality in sparse-view 3D reconstruction, reducing visible artifacts and recovering finer scene details.



### Modality-Shift Contrastive Decoding for Temporally Grounded Audio-Visual Understanding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34162v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.SD
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34162v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:56:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:56:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Minyeong Jeong, Yeonju Kim, Yong Man Ro
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Audio-visual large language models (AV-LLMs) are designed to represent the temporal structure of audio and video, yet it remains unclear how effectively they use this temporal information to relate sounds to visual events. To examine this, we probe an AV-LLM on an audio-visual synchronization task by shifting the audio so that the queried sound overlaps a different visual event. We observe that the model still favors the originally aligned event over the newly aligned one, indicating limited sensitivity to changes in audio-visual temporal alignment. To address this, we propose Modality-Shift Contrastive Decoding (MSCD), a training-free decoding method that emphasizes audio-visual temporal alignment in model predictions. MSCD contrasts logits under preserved and disrupted temporal alignment to favor predictions supported by aligned audio and video. We disrupt this alignment by temporally shifting either the audio or the video, while keeping the other modality fixed. Extensive evaluations with two AV-LLMs across three benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of MSCD for temporally grounded audio-visual understanding. In particular, MSCD improves accuracy over the base models on Daily-Omni by 2.2 and 2.8 percentage points for OmniVinci and video-SALMONN 2+, respectively, suggesting that emphasizing temporal alignment can also benefit broader audio-visual understanding.



### Admoab-DETR: Resolution-Tiered Fusion for Infrared-Visible Object Detection with Missing Modalities
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34163v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34163v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 10:56:33+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 10:56:33+00:00
- **Authors**: Wenchao Li, Haoran Jia, Shuyuan Wen, Keyan Chen
- **Comment**: Submitted to CVPR 2027
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Infrared and visible images offer complementary cues for all-weather object detection, yet real-world sensor dropouts can leave one stream unavailable and degrade paired-input detectors. Meanwhile, existing modality-adaptive methods apply heavy cross-modal attention uniformly across scales, incurring prohibitive token overhead on high-resolution maps. We propose Admoab-DETR, a unified real-time detector supporting infrared-only, visible-only, and paired inputs with a single set of weights. Specifically, \emph{Resolution-Tiered Fusion} (RTF) allocates fusion capacity by spatial token density and feature granularity: a lightweight Spatial Modality Gate governs fine local fusion at the highest-resolution scale with zero-cost single-view pass-through, while channel-compressed deformable Admoab necks capture cross-modal correspondence at coarser semantic scales. Furthermore, \emph{Mode-Matched Feature Distillation} (MMFD) aligns multi-scale encoder features with an availability-synchronized full-fusion teacher, avoiding ill-posed cross-modal hallucination while cutting parameters by 18.1\% and paired latency by 39.6\% at zero inference overhead. We also construct Admoab, a strictly registered 20,000-pair infrared--visible benchmark across diverse scenes. Experiments show Admoab-DETR achieves state-of-the-art accuracy--efficiency trade-offs across missing-modality and paired benchmarks. Our code will be available at https://github.com/liwenchao0615/Admoab-DETR.



### Agentic-Level Adversarial Purification: Revisiting Diffusion Purifiers on Native-Resolution Agentic Inputs
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34181v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CR**, cs.AI, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34181v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 11:10:06+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 11:10:06+00:00
- **Authors**: Yan Lin, Zhongliang Guo, Jingyu Lin, Daizong Liu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-language agents act on native-resolution renders of multi-element pages, where one perturbed photo or a few altered text strokes can redirect the decision. Any content provider can craft such a perturbation on the open model behind the agent, so a plug-in purifier is the natural first line of defense. Yet diffusion purifiers were designed and evaluated at the fixed resolution of the diffusion model on classifier or natural-image benchmarks, and defense evaluations for agents test JPEG, blur and noise but no diffusion purifier. We present the first study of adversarial purification at the agentic level, for an agent built on Qwen3-VL-8B: purifiers run at native resolution on multi-element inputs, face grey-box attackers who know the agent's model but not the defense, and are compared at matched clean cost. We show that treating agentic inputs as classifier inputs misleads both evaluation and design. Resizing the input to the resolution of the diffusion model, as existing purifiers do, raises the TextVQA clean cost of the Divide-and-Conquer purifier (DC) from 4.0 to 35.7 points. On web-shop pages, DC keeps 85.0% grey-box robust accuracy against web-hardened perturbations, where cheap input transformations at matched clean cost keep at most 40.0%, and this robustness comes from the global noise level: input-estimated masks and noise levels add none. These findings hold on a disjoint held-out set and on a second VLM and yield three requirements for defenses, we release the protocol.



### Loop4D: Embodied World Modeling with 4D VAEs and Looped Diffusion Transformers
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34199v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34199v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 11:27:05+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 11:27:05+00:00
- **Authors**: Gen Li, Fan Luo, Mengjun Lin, Shu Han, Bohan Li, Hao Zhao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Predicting the outcomes of robotic manipulation requires modeling both visual appearance and evolving 3D geometry. This poses two challenges: video compression can obscure fine-grained robot motion, while jointly predicting RGB and geometry demands sufficient generative capacity. We introduce Loop4D, a framework that addresses these challenges through motion-preserving 4D representations and recurrent generative refinement. Our 4D VAE encodes RGB and pointmap sequences into compact spatiotemporal latents while preserving motion details and geometric structure. Our RGB VAE combines geometry-guided supervision with predictive reconstruction to reduce compression-induced motion artifacts. Our geometry VAE uses lightweight adapters to adapt pretrained RGB representations while preserving their priors. Building on these representations, our Dual-Stream Looped Diffusion Transformer maintains separate appearance and geometry streams with shared Transformer weights and cross-modal connections. By reusing Transformer blocks across loops, we increase effective depth without replicating backbone parameters, enabling models with fewer parameters to improve joint RGB-geometry prediction through additional computation. Experiments demonstrate high-quality 4D generation and validate Loop4D in simulated robotic manipulation tasks.



### Beyond Logits: Coherent Hallucination Mitigation via Attention Contrastive Decoding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34207v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34207v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 11:32:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 11:32:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Yujia Chen, Rui Sun, Huayu Mai, Wangkai Li, Zhangyu He, Bingzhou Wang, Aibing Li, Wenzhang Sun, Tianzhu Zhang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) demonstrate impressive multimodal capabilities, yet suffer from hallucination - generating factually inaccurate content. Contrastive Decoding (CD) mitigates this by contrasting amateur and expert branches at the logit level. However, our investigation reveals that such logit-level interventions fundamentally compromise generation coherence, necessitating restrictive penalty constraints unrelated to hallucination suppression.   We introduce Attention Contrastive Decoding (ACD), a training-free plug-in that complements logit-level CD by relocating part of the contrastive operations to the attention mechanism. Operating at an earlier stage of the forward pass, ACD performs smooth semantic-preserving interventions through an Adaptive Subtraction Strategy (ASS), which attenuates hallucination-associated attention patterns while amplifying critical visual information. Extensive experiments demonstrate that combining ACD with existing CD methods (e.g., VCD+ACD) produces substantially more coherent outputs with further reduced hallucinations, eliminating restrictive penalties while enabling trustworthy multimodal generation.



### GeoCAM: Self-Supervised Sonar-Optical Representation Learning via Geographic Overlap Supervision
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34218v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34218v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 11:42:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 11:42:38+00:00
- **Authors**: Fadi Abdeladhim Zidi, Andrea De Salve, Giuseppe Celeste, Abdellah Zakaria Sellam, Paolo Spagnolo, Cosimo Distante
- **Comment**: 6 pages, 2 figures, 2 tables. Accepted at the IEEE International Conference on Advanced Visual and Signal-Based Systems (AVSS 2026)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Cross-modal self-supervised learning for paired side-scan sonar and underwater optical imagery is a promising direction for benthic habitat understanding, but it remains underexplored because existing methods do not fully exploit the one-to-many correspondences and geographic metadata available in real opti-acoustic surveys. This limits the ability to learn representations that are both semantically meaningful and geometrically consistent from abundant unlabeled marine data. To address this problem, we present GeoCAM, a geography-guided framework that learns a shared embedding space for sonar tiles and their associated optical views without semantic annotations. GeoCAM achieves its goal by combining three components: (i) a Set Attention Aggregator that adaptively summarizes multiple optical observations into a single tile-level representation; (ii) a geometry-weighted InfoNCE objective that converts GPS-derived footprint overlap into soft positive supervision; and (iii) an optional cross-modal masked reconstruction branch that encourages physically grounded cross-modal alignment. Experiments on the BenthiCat SSS-CAM dataset show that GeoCAM can be effectively exploited to learn which sonar and camera samples describe the same seabed patch, improving cross-modal retrieval and alignment over strong contrastive baselines. Code is available at https://github.com/FadiZidiDz/GeoCAM.



### Variation-aware Temporal Correspondence for Self-Supervised Video Representation Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.34220v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.34220v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-25 11:43:06+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-25 11:43:06+00:00
- **Authors**: Yang Liu, Qianqian Xu, Peisong Wen, Siran Dai, Qingming Huang
- **Comment**: Accepted by T-PAMI
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Self-supervised video representation learning has achieved remarkable progress by leveraging large-scale unlabeled video data. Recent methods commonly extend Masked Image Modeling (MIM) to Masked Video Modeling (MVM), where video representations are learned by capturing semantic information from temporal contexts during masked content recovery. Unlike static images, videos exhibit inherent temporal variation, as visual objects continuously change their positions, appearances, and motion states across frames. Therefore, MVM needs to preserve semantic consistency while capturing temporal changes of corresponding regions, which raises two central challenges: 1) how to establish reliable temporal correspondence across frames, and 2) how to formulate an appropriate representation learning objective for modeling both semantic consistency and temporal variation. To address these challenges, we propose Variation-aware Temporal Correspondence for video Representation learning (VT-CoRe) framework. For challenge 1), VT-CoRe introduces a sandwich sampling strategy to construct two-sided temporal references and a Patch Registration Module (PRM) to establish reliable patch-level correspondence.
